Supplement to "The level of self-organized criticality in oscillating Brownian motion: n {n} -consistency and stable Poisson-type convergence of the MLE"
Johannes Brutsche, Angelika Rohde, Johannes Brutsche, Angelika Rohde

Abstract
For some discretely observed path of oscillating Brownian motion with level of self-organized criticality ρ 0 \rho_{0} , we prove in the infill asymptotics that the MLE is n n -consistent, where n n denotes the sample size, and derive its limit distribution with respect to stable convergence. As the transition density of this homogeneous Markov process is not even continuous in ρ 0 \rho_{0} , the analysis is highly non-standard. Therefore, interesting and somewhat unexpected phenomena occur: The likelihood function splits into several components, each of them contributing very differently depending on how close the argument ρ \rho is to ρ 0 \rho_{0} . Correspondingly, the MLE is successively excluded to lay outside a compact set, a 1 / n 1/\sqrt{n} -neighborhood and finally a 1 / n 1/n -neighborhood of ρ 0 \rho_{0} asymptotically. The crucial argument to derive the stable convergence is to exploit the semimartingale structure of the sequential suitably rescaled local log-likelihood function (as a process in time). Both sequentially and as a process in ρ \rho , it exhibits a bivariate Poissonian behavior in the stable limit with its intensity being a multiple of the local time at ρ 0 \rho_{0} .



and

??
Mathematical Institute, University of Freiburg, ??, ??

1
Introduction

Let
X=(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be the Markov process solving the homogeneous stochastic differential equation (SDE)

\displaystyle dX_{t}=\sigma_{\rho}(X_{t})dW_{t},\qquad X_{0}=x_{0}\in\mathbb{R},

(1.1)

where
W
is a standard Brownian motion and for different (but arbitrary) numbers
\alpha,\beta>0
, the diffusion coefficient is given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rho}(x):=\begin{cases}\alpha,&\textrm{ if }x<\rho,\\
\beta,&\textrm{ if }x\geq\rho.\end{cases}

(1.2)

As shown in
Le Gall (1984)
, the SDE (
1.1
) possesses a unique strong solution. We call the parameter
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
the
level of self-organized criticality
. In physics and biology, such type of process naturally arises when describing diffusive motion in porous or highly inhomogeneous media. In contrast to traditional change-point models where the structural break in volatility occurs in time, its occurrence here depends purely on the state of the process
X
itself. For
\rho=0
, the process described by (
1.1
) has been named oscillating Brownian motion (OBM), introduced and first studied in
Keilson and Wellner (1978)
, where its transition semigroup is provided in particular. By Proposition
4.2
in
Blanchard, Röckner and
Russo (2010)
, the transition density
p_{t}^{\rho}(x,y)
from state
x
to state
y
within time
t
of the homogeneous Markov process
X
for general
\rho
is given by the unique solution of the Kolmogorov forward (or Fokker–Planck) equation

\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial y^{2}}\left(\sigma_{\rho}(y)^{2}p_{t}^{\rho}(x,y)\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial t}p_{t}^{\rho}(x,y)

(1.3)

in a distributional sense. Here, we are in one of the rare situations where the partial differential equation has an explicit solution that is given in (
3.1
). It is important to anticipate that this transition density is not continuous in the parameter
\rho
.

In this article, we develop the theory of the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE) for
\rho
on the basis of discrete observations

\displaystyle X_{i/n},\ \ i=1,\dots,n,

(1.4)

as
n\rightarrow\infty
. In this infill asymptotics or so-called high-frequency observation scheme, averaged squared estimators for
\alpha^{2}
and
\beta^{2}
have been studied in
Lejay and Pigato (2018)
when
\rho=0
is known. They especially establish stable convergence of their estimators towards Gaussian mixtures at
\sqrt{n}
-convergence rate. Likewise, when the level of self-organized criticality is known to the statistician,
Mazzonetto (2026)
proved very recently the convergence of suitable statistics to the local time. For so-called drifted OBM,
Lejay and Pigato (2020)
study maximum likelihood estimators for drift parameters in case
\rho=0
. Within the high-frequency literature, our study is complementary in the sense that we treat
\alpha
and
\beta
as given while inferring about
\rho
. We also quote the contributions
Kutoyants (2012)
,
Su and Chan (2015)
,
Su and Chan (2017)
, and
Mazzonetto and
Pigato (2024)
for inference in ergodic threshold diffusions as the time horizon of the observed trajectories goes to infinity, where estimation of the threshold is already an issue for a continuous record of observations if the threshold is solely present in the drift.
By the Markovian structure, the likelihood function for (
1.4
) factorizes into a product of transition densities such that the log-likelihood function has the form

L_{n}(x_{0},\dots,x_{n};\rho):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log\left(p_{1/n}^{\rho}(x_{k-1},x_{k})\right).

This function is not continuous in
\rho
(indeed it is not even upper semicontinuous), but càdlàg for the version (
3.1
) of the transition density. We define the MLE
\hat{\rho}_{n}
as some arbitrary but measurably selected representative of

\displaystyle\underset{\rho\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }L_{n}\left(X_{0},X_{1/n},\dots,X_{1};\rho\right),

where for any càdlàg function
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

\underset{x\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }f(x):=\bigg\{x\in\mathbb{R}:\ \max\Big\{\lim_{u\nearrow x,u<x}f(u),f(x)\Big\}=\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}f(y)\bigg\}.

Note that
\mathrm{Argsup}_{\rho}L_{n}(\rho)
is always a closed subset of
\mathbb{R}
but may be empty in which case we define
\hat{\rho}_{n}=0
. If it is not empty, a measurable selection always exists bei Proposition
2.8
in
Ferger (2015)
. We write
\arg\sup
if
\mathrm{Argsup}
is a singleton.
As the MLE hinges on the likelihood function, the discontinuity of the latter is precisely the reason why the MLE theory is getting highly non-standard and exhibits fascinating phenomena. For the mathematical analysis it turns out to be purposeful to rewrite
\hat{\rho}_{n}=\rho_{0}+\hat{\theta}_{n}
for the true parameter
\rho_{0}
, where

\hat{\theta}_{n}\in\underset{\theta\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log\left(p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})\right).

Thus,
\hat{\rho}_{n}\approx\rho_{0}
corresponds to
\hat{\theta}_{n}\approx 0
. Observe also that
\hat{\theta}_{n}\in\mathrm{Argsup}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}}\ell_{n}(\theta)
, where

\displaystyle\ell_{n}(\theta):=\ell_{n}(X_{0},X_{1/n},\dots,X_{1};\theta):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log\Bigg(\frac{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})}{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n)}}\Bigg).

(1.5)

Due to the four regimes
\{x<\rho,y\leq\rho\},\{x\geq\rho,y>\rho\},\{x<\rho<y\}
, and
\{y\leq\rho\leq x\}
appearing in the transition density (
3.1
), every summand in
\ell_{n}(\theta)
splits into nine disjoint regimes which are listed in (
3.2
). Correspondingly,
\ell_{n}(\theta)
itself admits a decomposition into nine sums and, impressively, the nature of the MLE is characterized by their subtle interplay. Which of these sums dominantly impact the shape and ’randomness’ of
\ell_{n}(\theta)
depends crucially on whether
\theta\gg 1/\sqrt{n}
,
\theta\asymp 1/\sqrt{n}
or
\theta\ll 1/\sqrt{n}
. Realizations of the log-likelihood function are depicted in Figure
1.1
. Two characteristic features immediately catch the eye:

•

the triangular shape in a neighborhood of
\rho_{0}
(corresponding to
\theta=0
),

•

the jumps within the
1/n
-environment of
\rho_{0}
with piecewise linear behavior in between.

Fig 1.1:
Different realizations of the log-likelihood function for
n=10,000
, based on a path of
X
simulated for the parameters
\alpha=0.5
,
\beta=0.7
and
\rho_{0}=0
on a grid of the interval
[0,1]
with gridsize
10^{-6}
.
(a)
A path in the ’global’ environment.
(b)
A path in the
1/\sqrt{n}
-neighborhood.
(c)
A path in the
1/n
-neighborhood.
(d)
An average over
50
realizations of the likelihood in the
1/n
-neighborhood.

To formally state the result, define two independent standard Poisson processes
N
and
N^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
on a suitable so-called very good extension of the original probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
that are independent of
W
, see Section
2
for details. Here, we restrict attention to the case of
\mathcal{F}
being the completed
\sigma
-field generated by
W
. Then we define a process
\ell=(\ell(z))_{z\in\mathbb{R}}
via

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell(z)&:=\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\geq 0\}}\left(b_{\alpha,\beta}-\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\right)+\mathbbm{1}_{\{z<0\}}\left(b_{\alpha,\beta}^{\prime}-\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\log\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\right)\right)\right)|z|\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt+\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\geq 0\}}\log(\beta^{2}/\alpha^{2})N(z/\beta^{2})+\mathbbm{1}_{\{z<0\}}\log(\alpha^{2}/\beta^{2})N^{\prime}((-z/\alpha^{2})-)\right),\end{split}

(1.6)

where the constants
b_{\alpha,\beta},b_{\alpha,\beta}^{\prime}<0
are given explicitly in (
3.8
). Note that
\ell(z)
is the sum of a two-sided compensated Poisson process and a negative drift, where we have used the left-continuous version
N^{\prime}(\bullet-)
to ensure
\ell
being càdlàg. For statistical purposes, we even prove
\mathcal{F}
-stable convergence (denoted as
\mathcal{F}
-
st
) of the MLE instead of mere convergence in law. This notion of convergence was first introduced in
Rényi (1963)
and further studied in
Aldous and Eagleson (1978)
, see Section
2
for a detailed description. Denoting by
L_{t}^{\rho}(X)
the local time of
X
in
\rho
at time
t
, we are now in a position to state the main result of this article.

Theorem 1.1
.

On the extended probability space,

n\left(\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{F}-st}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ \underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X))\mathbbm{1}_{\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}},

where the right-hand side is a well-defined
\mathbb{R}
-valued random variable almost surely.

Statistical consequence

Note that both the intensity of the two-sided Poisson process and the drift of the process on the right-hand side in Theorem
1.1
are given as multiples of the local time
L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)
. As concerns statistical applications, however,
L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)
is not observable and here the
\mathcal{F}
-stable convergence is the way out: Due to this stronger mode of convergence as compared to convergence in distribution, both sides in Theorem
1.1
can be multiplied by the
\mathcal{F}
-measurable local time
L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)
and the convergence is still in place. By further replacing the local time by a local time estimator, we are even able to derive a limit that does not depend on any unknown quantities any longer. To be more precise, let
(\hat{L}_{n}^{\rho})_{\rho\in\mathbb{R}}
be a family of estimators satisfying

\displaystyle\hat{L}_{n}^{\hat{\rho}_{n}}\longrightarrow_{\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}}L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X).

(1.7)

Here, the subscript
\rho_{0}
in the probability
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}
indicates that
X
solves (
1.1
) with
\rho=\rho_{0}
. Based on
Mazzonetto (2026)
, an example of local time estimators satisfying (
1.7
) is given in Section
C
of the supplement. The special structure of the limit
\ell
reveals the distributional identity

\mathcal{L}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\left.L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)\ \underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell\big(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)\big)\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)=\mathcal{L}\left(\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell(z)\right),

and the stable convergence in Theorem
1.1
together with the stochastic convergence in (
1.7
) and Theorem
3.18
(b) in
Häusler and
Luschgy (2015)
yield the joint stable convergence

\Big(\hat{L}_{n}^{\hat{\rho}_{n}},n\left(\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}}\Big)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{F}-st}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Big(L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X),\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X))\mathbbm{1}_{\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}}\Big).

Together with the (stable) continuous mapping theorem and Theorem
3.17
(iv) in
Häusler and
Luschgy (2015)
, this implies weak convergence of the conditional laws

\mathcal{L}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\left.n\hat{L}_{n}^{\hat{\rho}_{n}}\big(\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}\big)\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)\Longrightarrow\mathcal{L}\left(\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell(z)\right).

Note that the right-hand side is independent of
X
and
\rho_{0}
. Let
\kappa\in(0,1)
be given and denote by
q_{\kappa/2}
and
q_{1-\kappa/2}
the
\kappa/2
- and
(1-\kappa/2)
-quantile of
\arg\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\ell(z)
, respectively. Then

\displaystyle\left[\hat{\rho}_{n}-\frac{1}{n\hat{L}_{n}^{\hat{\rho}_{n}}}q_{1-\kappa/2},\hat{\rho}_{n}-\frac{1}{n\hat{L}_{n}^{\hat{\rho}_{n}}}q_{\kappa/2}\right]

(1.8)

is an asymptotic
(1-\kappa)
-confidence interval for
\rho_{0}
conditional on
\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}
by means of Theorem
1.1
(which should be read as
\mathbb{R}
in case
\hat{L}_{n}^{\hat{\rho}_{n}}=0
). Note that
\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)=0\}
coincides with the event where the process
(X_{t})_{0\leq t\leq 1}
has not crossed the level
\rho_{0}
according to Corollary
29.18
in
Kallenberg (2021)
. An unconditional confidence set can be obtained by first testing the null hypothesis
L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)=0
and adjusting the confidence level accordingly.

Overview of the proof

Central for the strategy of proof of Theorem
1.1
is the characteristic property

n(\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0})\in\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }\ell_{n}(z/n).

Correspondingly, the proof contains two major steps, which are stated in the following two propositions.

Proposition 1.2
.

The MLE
\hat{\rho}_{n}
is
n
-consistent on
\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}
, i.e.

\lim_{K\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\big(n\left|\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}\right|>K,L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\big)=0.

Proposition 1.3
.

For any
K>0
, we have

\displaystyle\left(\ell_{n}(z/n)\right)_{z\in[-K,K]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{F}-st}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ \left(\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X))\right)_{z\in[-K,K]}

in the Skorohod space
\mathcal{D}([-K,K])
.

Based on those results, the proof of Theorem
1.1
is then completed with argsup-continuous mapping type arguments.

The proof of Proposition
1.2
adopts the M-estimation approach, c.f. Section
3.2
in
van der Vaart and
Wellner (2023)
. To this aim, the log-likelihood function
\ell_{n}
is decomposed into a drift
B_{n}
and a sum of martingale differences
M_{n}
by adding and subtracting the sum of conditional expectations of each of its increments with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{(k-1)/n}
, see (
3.4
). While the martingale part
M_{n}
consists of martingale increments which are convenient to work with, the problem of this natural decomposition is the randomness of the drift term
B_{n}
. This prevents us from directly applying Markov’s inequality in the slicing device, where expressions of the form
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\big(\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}M_{n}(\theta)>-\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}B_{n}(\theta)|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\big)
need to be bounded for suitably chosen partitions
S_{n,j},j\in\mathbb{N}
, of the parameter space. Instead, we first deduce deterministic bounds for
\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}B_{n}(\theta)\mathbbm{1}_{A(n)}
on certain sets
A(n)
with high probability that require a precise understanding of the drift term. Here, the particular difficulty is caused by the decomposition of
\ell_{n}(\theta)
into nine disjoint regimes which contribute to the drift rather differently depending on how close
\theta
is to zero, see Figure
4.1
. Correspondingly, the MLE is successively excluded to lay outside a compact set, a
1/\sqrt{n}
-neighborhood and finally a
1/n
-neighborhood of
\rho_{0}
asymptotically. For
\theta
small enough, Proposition
3.1
provides an expansion that mimics the triangular shape of
\ell_{n}
close to the true parameter seen in Figure
1.1
, provided that
L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0
. Already for larger values of
\theta
in the
1/\sqrt{n}
-environment we are facing the problem that due the
n
-dependence of the step size in the transition density, the remainder terms in Taylor expansions within regimes (that Proposition
3.1
is built on) are of the same order as the leading terms and are therefore not useful any longer. As Figure
1.1
shows, the triangular shape is indeed globally not valid.

The proof of Proposition
1.3
requires to prove
\mathcal{F}
-stable convergence of finite dimensional distributions (fidis) and tightness. Unlike in many situations, the much more delicate part is to establish stable convergence of fidis, whereas tightness is a consequence of the standard moment criterion for tightness in the Skorohod space, combined with a thorough understanding of
\ell_{n}
, in particular the moments bounds provided in Proposition
3.4
. By means of the Cramér–Wold device, convergence of fidis is a consequence of convergence of arbitrary linear combinations of
\ell_{n}(z/n)
for different
z
. As the process
(\ell_{n}(z/n))_{z\in\mathbb{R}}
has no specific structure in its parameter
z
, for example being Markovian or a martingale, the crucial idea is to artificially consider the sequential process

\displaystyle\ell_{n,t}(z/n)=\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\log\left(\frac{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+z/n}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})}{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n)}}\right),\qquad t\in[0,1],

(1.9)

as a process in time and use its specific semimartingale decomposition given in (
3.4
) with respect to the discretized filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{\lfloor nt\rfloor/n})_{t\in[0,1]}
.
Jacod (1997)
provides a stable convergence result in exactly this setting of infill asymptotics, but only for conditional Gaussian limits.
Jacod (2003)
describes stable convergence towards a more general class of processes that covers Poisson limits, but in its current formulation, this result does not apply to processes defined on discretized filtrations. We therefore adapt this result by combining it with the first one to bridge the gap between those two, see Proposition
H.1
. Based on this, proving stable convergence of fidis is traced back to proving (uniform) stochastic convergence of the semimartingale characteristics of the sequential version of any linear combination
\kappa_{1}\ell_{n}(z_{1}/n)+\dots+\kappa_{N}\ell_{n}(z_{N}/n)
. Fascinatingly, only two of the nine regimes in (
3.2
) contribute to the stochastic fluctuation of
\ell_{n}(z/n)
asymptotically. The resulting limit can then be constructed based on a bivariate Poisson process as we observe Poissonian behavior both in time
t
and location
z
. Remarkably, it turns out that
(\ell(z))_{z\geq 0}
indeed is given as a martingale plus drift, whereas we did no have a martingale structure of
\ell_{n}(z/n)
in
z
.

The article is organized as follows: On the basis of Propositions
1.2
and
1.3
, the proof of Theorem
1.1
is conducted in Section
2
. Section
3
contains important features of the log-likelihood function, including the explicit form of the transition density, the above mentioned nine regimes, the martingale and drift decomposition of
\ell_{n}
together with the central expansion of the drift and moment bounds for increments of
\ell_{n}
. The proof of Proposition
1.2
is given in Section
4
. Section
5
contains the proof of Proposition
1.3
, including a heuristic reasoning for the Poissonian structure of the limit and an explicit construction of
(\ell(z))_{z\in\mathbb{R}}
. Remaining auxiliary results, contained in the Appendix Sections
A
-
I
, are deferred to the supplement.

2
Proof of Theorem
1.1

In this section the proof of Theorem
1.1
is conducted on the basis of Proposition
1.2
and
1.3
. The property of the limit is outsourced to Lemma
2.1
.

Preliminaries on stable convergence

Let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space supporting a standard Brownian motion
W
with
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
being the augmented filtration induced by
W
and restrict attention to the case
\mathcal{F}=\mathcal{F}_{1}
. Let
(\Omega^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime},(\mathcal{F}_{t}^{\prime})_{t\geq 0})
be the canonical space of all
\mathbb{R}^{2}
-valued càdlàg functions on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
with the canonical process
(N(z),N(z)^{\prime})(\omega^{\prime})=\omega^{\prime}(z)
and the right-continuous filtration generated by
(N,N^{\prime})
. Furthermore, let
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
be the unique probability measure on
(\Omega^{\prime},\mathcal{F}^{\prime})
under which the processes
N
and
N^{\prime}
are independent standard Poisson processes.
We then define a stochastic basis
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}=(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},(\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{t})_{t\geq 0},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
via

\displaystyle\tilde{\Omega}:=\Omega\times\Omega^{\prime},\quad\tilde{\mathcal{F}}:=\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{F}^{\prime},\quad\tilde{\mathcal{F}}_{t}:=\bigcap_{s>t}\mathcal{F}_{s}\otimes\mathcal{F}_{s}^{\prime},\quad\tilde{\mathbb{P}}:=\mathbb{P}\otimes\mathbb{P}^{\prime}.

(2.1)

It is clear in this case that the extension is
very good
(in the sense of Definition
II.7.1
in
Jacod and Shiryaev (2003)
), meaning that every martingale on
\mathcal{B}
is also a martingale on
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
. Finally, we can define
(\ell(z))_{z\in\mathbb{R}}
as given in (
1.6
) on the stochastic basis
\tilde{\mathcal{B}}
.

Let
(Z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be a sequence of random variables with values in a metric space
E
and defined on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
and
Z
an
E
-valued random variable on
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
. Then we say that
(Z_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges
\mathcal{F}
-stably in law
to
Z
if

\mathbb{E}\left[Vf(Z_{n})\right]\longrightarrow\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\left[Vf(Z)\right],

for all
f:E\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
bounded and continuous and all bounded random variables
V
on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F})
, where
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
denotes the expectation with respect to
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
. This property is (slightly) stronger than mere convergence in law. It applies in particular for
E
being the Skorohod space
\mathcal{D}([0,1])
.

Lemma 2.1
.

Provided that
L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0
, the set
\mathrm{Argsup}_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X))
is
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
-almost surely non-empty and a singleton.

The equality
\ell(0)=0
reveals the upper bound

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\Big(\mathrm{Argsup}_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\ell\big(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)\big)=\emptyset,L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\Big)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\left(\bigcap_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\bigg\{\sup_{|z|>2^{M}}\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X))\geq 0\bigg\}\cap\big\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\big\}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\lim_{M\to\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\sup_{|z|>2^{M}}\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X))\geq 0,L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\bigg).

By disintegration, construction of
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
in (
2.1
), independence of
N,N^{\prime}
and
X
and monotone convergence,

\displaystyle\lim_{M\to\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\sup_{|z|>2^{M}}\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X))\geq 0,L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\bigg)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\lim_{M\to\infty}\int_{(0,\infty)}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\sup_{|z|>2^{M}}\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X))\geq 0\bigg|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)=c\bigg)\tilde{\mathbb{P}}^{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)}(dc)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\int_{(0,\infty)}\lim_{M\to\infty}\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\sup_{|z|>2^{M}}\ell(cz)\geq 0\bigg)\mathbb{P}^{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)}(dc).

Recall that under
\mathbb{P}^{\prime}
, both
N
and
N^{\prime}
are independent standard Poisson processes. Then we obtain by the union bound in the first step and Doob’s maximal inequality in the second one,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\left(\sup_{z>2^{M}}\ell(cz)\geq 0\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\geq M}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\left(\sup_{z\in(2^{j},2^{j+1}]}\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\left(N(cz/\beta^{2})-\frac{cz}{\beta^{2}}\right)\geq-b_{\alpha,\beta}c2^{j}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\frac{2}{(-b_{\alpha,\beta})c}\sum_{j\geq M}2^{-j}\mathbb{E}^{\prime}\left[\left(N(c2^{j+1}/\beta^{2})-\frac{c2^{j+1}}{\beta^{2}}\right)^{2}\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\frac{2}{(-b_{\alpha,\beta})\beta\sqrt{c}}\sum_{j\geq M}2^{-j}2^{(j+1)/2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle M\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0

for any
c>0
. The case
z\leq-2^{M}
can be dealt with analogously and it follows that the random set
\mathrm{Argsup}_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X))
is non-empty almost surely.
For the uniqueness we obtain again by disintegration as above

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\#\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X))>1,L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\bigg)\hskip-0.56905pt=\hskip-0.56905pt\int_{(0,\infty)}\hskip-5.69054pt\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\bigg(\#\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }\ell(cz)>1\bigg)\mathbb{P}^{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)}(dc)

and distinguish cases to prove that the integrand is equal to zero:

\bullet\ \boldsymbol{\#([0,\infty)\cap\mathrm{Argsup}_{z}\ell(cz))\geq 2}
.

Let
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathrm{Argsup}_{z}\ell(cz)
with
z_{1},z_{2}\geq 0
. By monotonicity of the (deterministic) first summand in the definition of
\ell(cz)
, it follows that
N(cz_{i}/\beta^{2})-N(cz_{i}/\beta^{2}-)\neq 0
for
i=1,2
. Moreover, as the Poisson process
N
takes only values in
\mathbb{N}_{0}
we obtain that
\ell(cz_{1})=\ell(cz_{2})
is only possible for
z_{1}-z_{2}\in\kappa_{\alpha,\beta}\mathbb{Z}
for suitable
\kappa_{\alpha,\beta}
.
Let
T_{n}
be the
n
-th jump time of
N(c/\beta^{2})
. Then
T_{n}-T_{m}
,
m<n
, is gamma distributed with parameters
n-m
and
c/\beta^{2}
, and we conclude

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\left(\#\Big([0,\infty)\cap\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }\ell(cz)\Big)>1\right)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\left(\bigcup_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}:\ m<n}\left\{T_{n}-T_{m}\in\kappa_{\alpha,\beta}\mathbb{Z}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{m,n\in\mathbb{N}:\ m<n}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\left(T_{n}-T_{m}\in\kappa_{\alpha,\beta}\mathbb{Z}\right)=0,

as the probability of a gamma distributed random variable being in some countable set is zero.

\bullet\ \boldsymbol{\#((-\infty,0]\cap\mathrm{Argsup}_{z}\ell(cz))\geq 2}
.

This follows as in the previous case.

\bullet\ \boldsymbol{\#(-\infty,0]\cap\mathrm{Argsup}_{z}\ell(cz))\geq 1,\#([0,\infty)\cap\mathrm{Argsup}_{z}\ell(cz))\geq 1}
.

Let
z_{1}\geq 0
and
z_{2}<0
with
z_{1},z_{2}\in\mathrm{Argsup}_{z}\ell(cz)
. As in the first case,
N
jumps at
z_{1}
and
N^{\prime}
at
z_{2}
. As both
N
and
N^{\prime}
only take values in
\mathbb{N}_{0}
, for each
z_{1}
there exists a countable set
A(z_{1})
such that
\ell(cz_{1})=\ell(cz_{2})
is only valid for
z_{2}\in A(z_{1})
. Denoting by
T_{n}^{1},T_{n}^{\prime}
the
n
-th jump time of
N(c\bullet/\beta^{2})
and
N^{\prime}(c\bullet/\alpha^{2})
, respectively, we obtain from independence of
N
and
N^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\left(\#\Big((-\infty,0]\cap\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }\ell(cz)\Big)\geq 1,\#\Big([0,\infty)\cap\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }\ell(cz)\Big)\geq 1\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\left(\left.\bigcup_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\{T_{m}^{\prime}\in A(z_{1})\}\right|T_{n}=z_{1}\right)(\mathbb{P}^{\prime})^{T_{n}}(dz_{1})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\sum_{m,n=1}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}^{\prime}\left(T_{m}^{\prime}\in A(z_{1})\right)(\mathbb{P}^{\prime})^{T_{n}}(dz_{1})=0.

∎

Recall
n(\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0})\in\mathrm{Argsup}_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\ell_{n}(z/n)
. By Proposition
1.2
, the sequence
(n(\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight and by Proposition
1.3
and Lemma
3
in Section
16
of
Billingsley (1999)
we have

\displaystyle\left(\ell_{n}(z/n)\right)_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{F}-st}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ \left(\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X))\right)_{z\in\mathbb{R}}.

(2.2)

Here, the Skorohod space
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R})
is endowed with the topology of Skorohod convergence on compact sets. Let
F\in\mathcal{F}
be a set with
\mathbb{P}(F)>0
. Recalling that (
2.2
) is given on the extended probability space
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
defined in (
2.1
) and denoting
A:=\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}
, we define
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{A\cap F}
via

\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{A\cap F}(B):=\frac{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}((A\cap F)\cap B)}{\tilde{\mathbb{P}}(A\cap F)}

for any
B\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
and the identification of
A\cap F
with the set
(A\cap F)\times\Omega^{\prime}\in\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
. As the convergence (
2.2
) is
\mathcal{F}
-stable, by Theorem
3.17
(iv) in
Häusler and
Luschgy (2015)
, we find

\left(\ell_{n}(z/n)\right)_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{A\cap F}}}{{\Longrightarrow}}\ \left(\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X))\right)_{z\in\mathbb{R}},

where
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{A\cap F}}}{{\Longrightarrow}}
denotes weak convergence of random variables with respect to the measure
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{A\cap F}
. Clearly,
(n(\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0})\mathbbm{1}_{\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is also tight with respect to
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{A\cap F}
and by Lemma
2.1
the Argsup of
(\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)))_{z\in\mathbb{R}}
is a singleton in
\mathbb{R}
almost surely with respect to this measure
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}_{A\cap F}
. Hence, Theorem
3.12
in
Ferger (2015)
yields for any bounded and continuous function
f:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,

\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{A\cap F}f\left(\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell_{n}(z/n)\right)\right]\longrightarrow\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{A\cap F}f\left(\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X))\right)\right].

From this, we obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{F}f\left(\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell_{n}(z/n)\mathbbm{1}_{A}\right)\right]\longrightarrow\tilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\mathbbm{1}_{F}f\left(\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X))\mathbbm{1}_{A}\right)\right].

By Theorem
3.17
(iv) in
Häusler and
Luschgy (2015)
, this implies

\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell_{n}(z/n)\mathbbm{1}_{\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{F}-st}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ \underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell(zL_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X))\mathbbm{1}_{\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}}

and the claim of Theorem
1.1
follows.
∎

3
The log-likelihood function and its fundamental properties

The transition density
p_{t}^{\rho}(x,y)
of the Markov process solving the SDE (
1.1
) is given as

\displaystyle p_{t}^{\rho}(x,y)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}\alpha}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{(y-x)^{2}}{2t\alpha^{2}}\right)-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{\alpha+\beta}\exp\left(-\frac{(y-2\rho+x)^{2}}{2t\alpha^{2}}\right)\right]&\textrm{ for }x<\rho,y\leq\rho,\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}\beta}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{(y-x)^{2}}{2t\beta^{2}}\right)+\frac{\alpha-\beta}{\alpha+\beta}\exp\left(-\frac{(y-2\rho+x)^{2}}{2t\beta^{2}}\right)\right]&\textrm{ for }x\geq\rho,y>\rho,\\
\frac{2}{\alpha+\beta}\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2t}\left(\frac{y-\rho}{\beta}-\frac{x-\rho}{\alpha}\right)^{2}\right)&\textrm{ for }x<\rho<y,\\
\frac{2}{\alpha+\beta}\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2t}\left(\frac{y-\rho}{\alpha}-\frac{x-\rho}{\beta}\right)^{2}\right)&\textrm{ for }y\leq\rho\leq x.\\
\end{cases}

(3.1)

Note that
p_{t}^{\rho}(x,y)=p_{t}^{0}(x-\rho,y-\rho)
. It is easily verified that
p_{t}^{\rho}(x,y)
solves (
1.3
), i.e.

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{t}^{\rho}(x,y)\varphi(y)dy=\varphi(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{\rho}(y)^{2}p_{s}^{\rho}(x,y)\varphi^{\prime\prime}(y)dyds

for every
t>0
and any
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, where
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
denotes the Schwartz space of rapidly decreasing functions on
\mathbb{R}
.
Corresponding to the four regimes in the transition density (
3.1
),

p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})\Big/p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})

is decomposed according to nine different regimes. For
\theta^{\prime}\leq\theta
, those are given explicitly as

\displaystyle\begin{split}&I_{1,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime},X_{k/n}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}\},\\
&I_{2,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}<X_{k/n}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta\},\\
&I_{3,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta<X_{k/n}\},\\
&I_{4,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{X_{k/n}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}\leq X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+\theta\},\\
&I_{5,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}\leq X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+\theta,\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}<X_{k/n}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta\},\\
&I_{6,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}\leq X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+\theta<X_{k/n}\},\\
&I_{7,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{X_{k/n}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta\leq X_{(k-1)/n}\},\\
&I_{8,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}<X_{k/n}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta\leq X_{(k-1)/n}\},\\
&I_{9,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{\rho_{0}+\theta\leq X_{(k-1)/n},\rho_{0}+\theta<X_{k/n}\}.\end{split}

(3.2)

Note that all those regimes are (pairwise) disjoint. If
\theta^{\prime}=0
, we write
I_{j,k}^{\theta}=I_{j,k}^{0,\theta}
. In particular,

\displaystyle\ell_{n}(\theta)=\sum_{j=1}^{9}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log\left(\frac{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})}{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n)}}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{I_{j,k}^{\theta}}=:\sum_{j=1}^{9}\mathcal{I}_{j}(\theta).

(3.3)

This is the decomposition of
\ell_{n}(\theta)
into nine sums as already mentioned in the introduction and constitues the origin of the fascinating phenomena within the analysis of the MLE. Regimes and their correspondences are depicted in Figure
3.1
. Interestingly, there are five different probabilistic types of contributions.

Fig 3.1:
Visualization of regimes (
3.2
) corresponding to the respective indicators in the nine summands in (
3.3
). Regimes which will turn out to be treated analogously are colored the same. Moreover, regimes contributing to the drift and variance in the
1/n
-neighborhood are marked with north-east and north-west lines, respectively.

Recall the sequential log-likelihood process
\ell_{n,t}(\theta)
given in (
1.9
), for which we have
\ell_{n,1}(\theta)=\ell_{n}(\theta)
, where
\ell_{n}(\theta)
is given in (
1.5
). It turns out to be purposeful to decompose

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{n,t}(\theta)=M_{n,t}(\theta)+B_{n,t}(\theta)\end{split}

(3.4)

into a martingale term and a drift term by adding and subtracting the sum of the
\mathcal{F}_{(k-1)/n}
-conditional expectation of each increment of
\ell_{n,t}(\theta)
, i.e.

B_{n,t}(\theta)=\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\left.\log\left(\frac{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})}{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n)}}\right)\right|X_{(k-1)/n}\right].

Note that
(M_{n,t})_{t\in[0,1]}
is actually a martingale with respect to the discretized filtration
(\mathcal{F}_{\lfloor nt\rfloor/n})_{t\in[0,1]}
.
The
t
-dependent version of
\ell_{n}(\theta)
plays a central role in the study of the limiting distribution, whereas the
n
-consistency only uses
\ell_{n}(\theta)=M_{n}(\theta)+B_{n}(\theta)
, where
M_{n}(\theta):=M_{n,1}(\theta)
and
B_{n}(\theta):=B_{n,1}(\theta)
, correspondingly. Nevertheless, we will exploit the fact that
M_{n}(\theta)
is a sum of martingale differences for the
n
-consistency proof, where the (negative) drift
B_{n}(\theta)
is shown to dominate the stochastic fluctuations for
\theta
being not too close to
0
. Note that
B_{n}(\theta)\leq 0
follows direcly by Jensen’s inequality for conditional expectation, whereas it will turn out to be challenging to bound it away from zero, and especially sufficiently far away from zero, for general
\theta
. For
\theta
with
|\theta|\leq K/\sqrt{n}
with
K>0
arbitrary, we deduce an expansion for
B_{n,t}(\theta)
. Defining two numerical constants

\displaystyle F_{\alpha,\beta}:=-\left(\frac{2(\alpha-\beta)}{\alpha\beta}+\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\frac{2}{\alpha+\beta}\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\right)

(3.5)

and

\displaystyle\tilde{F}_{\alpha,\beta}:=-\left(\frac{2(\beta-\alpha)}{\alpha\beta}+\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\frac{2}{\alpha+\beta}\log\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\right)\right)

(3.6)

this expansion reads as follows.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
K>0
and
|\theta|\leq K/\sqrt{n}
. Then we have

\displaystyle B_{n,t}(\theta)=-|\theta|\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\{\theta\geq 0\}}F_{\alpha,\beta}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{\theta<0\}}\tilde{F}_{\alpha,\beta}\right)\Lambda_{\alpha,\beta}^{n}\left((X_{(k-1)/n})_{1\leq k\leq\lfloor nt\rfloor}\right)+r_{n}(t,\theta),

where

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Lambda_{\alpha,\beta}^{n}\left((X_{(k-1)/n})_{1\leq k\leq\lfloor nt\rfloor}\right)&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi/n}}\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\mathbbm{1}_{\{X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}\}}\exp\left(-\frac{(X_{(k-1)/n}-\rho_{0})^{2}}{2\alpha^{2}/n}\right)\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi/n}}\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}\mathbbm{1}_{\{X_{(k-1)/n}\geq\rho_{0}\}}\exp\left(-\frac{(X_{(k-1)/n}-\rho_{0})^{2}}{2\beta^{2}/n}\right)\end{split}

(3.7)

and

\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\sup_{|\theta^{\prime}|\leq|\theta|}\sup_{s\leq t}\frac{|r_{n}(s,\theta^{\prime})|}{|\theta^{\prime}|}\right]\leq C_{\alpha,\beta}(K)|\theta|n^{3/2}\sqrt{t},

for some constant
C_{\alpha,\beta}(K)>0
that is independent of
n,t
and
\theta
.

The proof, which is deferred to Section
F
in the supplementary material, reveals that the constants
F_{\alpha,\beta},\tilde{F}_{\alpha,\beta}
are composed of the terms belonging to
I_{1,k}^{\theta},I_{2,k}^{\theta},I_{3,k}^{\theta}
and
I_{7,k}^{\theta},I_{8,k}^{\theta},I_{9,k}^{\theta}
. Note that these constants are strictly greater than zero for
\alpha\neq\beta
. Moreover, as they are different, the triangular shape described by

-|\theta|\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\{\theta\geq 0\}}F_{\alpha,\beta}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{\theta<0\}}\tilde{F}_{\alpha,\beta}\right)\Lambda_{\alpha,\beta}^{n}\left((X_{(k-1)/n})_{1\leq k\leq\lfloor nt\rfloor}\right)

is not symmetric around
\theta=0
, which matches Figure
1.1
. The steepness of the triangle depends on the magnitude of
\Lambda_{\alpha,\beta}^{n}
. The next lemma shows that
\Lambda_{\alpha,\beta}^{n}/n
converges in probability to a multiple of
L_{t}^{\rho_{0}}(X)
, uniformly in time.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
X=(X_{t})_{t\in[0,1]}
be a solution to (
1.1
) with diffusion parameter (
1.2
) and let
f:\mathbb{R}\longrightarrow\mathbb{R}
be a measurable bounded function such that
\int_{\mathbb{R}}|x|^{k}f(x)dx<\infty
for
k=0,1,2
. Then for every
\epsilon>0
we have

\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{t\in[0,1]}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor nt\rfloor}f\left(\sqrt{n}(X_{(k-1)/n}-\rho_{0})\right)-\lambda_{\alpha,\beta,\rho}(f)L_{t}^{\rho_{0}}(X)\right|>\epsilon\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ 0,

where

\lambda_{\alpha,\beta}(f):=\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\int_{-\infty}^{0}f(x)dx+\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\int_{0}^{\infty}f(x)dx.

The proof for
\rho_{0}=0
follows the proof of Lemma
4.3
in
Lejay and Pigato (2018)
(see also
Jacod (1998)
). For general
\rho_{0}
define
\tilde{X}_{t}:=X_{t}-\rho_{0}
.
Then
\tilde{X}_{0}=x_{0}-\rho_{0}
and

d\tilde{X}_{t}=dX_{t}=\sigma_{\rho_{0}}(X_{t})dW_{t}=\sigma_{\rho_{0}}(\tilde{X}_{t}+\rho_{0})dW_{t}.

The statement of the proposition now follows from the case
\rho_{0}=0
by noting that
L_{t}^{\rho_{0}}(X)=L_{t}^{0}(\tilde{X})
,
f(\sqrt{n}(X_{t}-\rho_{0}))=f(\sqrt{n}\tilde{X}_{t})
and

\sigma_{\rho_{0}}(\tilde{X}_{t}+\rho_{0})=\alpha\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tilde{X}_{t}+\rho_{0}<\rho_{0}\}}+\beta\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tilde{X}_{t}+\rho_{0}\geq\rho_{0}\}}=\alpha\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tilde{X}_{t}<0\}}+\beta\mathbbm{1}_{\{\tilde{X}_{t}\geq 0\}}=\sigma_{0}(\tilde{X}_{t}).

∎

Proposition
3.1
and Lemma
3.2
applied to
f=f_{\alpha,\beta}
with

f_{\alpha,\beta}(x)=\mathbbm{1}_{(-\infty,0)}(x)\exp(-x^{2}/(2\alpha^{2}))+\mathbbm{1}_{[0,\infty)}(x)\exp(-x^{2}/(2\beta^{2}))

yield the following limiting drift of
(\ell_{n,t}(z/n))_{t\in[0,1]}
:

Corollary 3.3
.

With

\displaystyle\begin{split}b_{\alpha,\beta}&:=F_{\alpha,\beta}\lambda_{\alpha,\beta}\big(f_{\alpha,\beta}\big)=\frac{\alpha^{2}-\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}\beta^{2}}+\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right),\\
b_{\alpha,\beta}^{\prime}&:=\tilde{F}_{\alpha,\beta}\lambda_{\alpha,\beta}\big(f_{\alpha,\beta}\big)=\frac{\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2}}{\alpha^{2}\beta^{2}}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\log\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\right),\end{split}

(3.8)

we have for any
K>0
,

\sup_{z\in[-K,K]}\sup_{s\leq t}\left|B_{n,s}(z/n)-|z|\left(\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\geq 0\}}b_{\alpha,\beta}+\mathbbm{1}_{\{z<0\}}b_{\alpha,\beta}^{\prime}\right)L_{s}^{\rho_{0}}(X)\right|\longrightarrow_{\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}}0.

Note that
b_{\alpha,\beta},b_{\alpha,\beta}^{\prime}<0
for
\alpha\neq\beta
. Finally, we prove a moment bound on the summands that appear in the definition of
\ell_{n}(\theta)
and
B_{n}(\theta)
. We already incorporate in the statement that parts of
\mathcal{I}_{2}(\theta)
and
\mathcal{I}_{8}(\theta)
equal
\pm\log(\beta^{2}/\alpha^{2})
on their respective interval which are the only contributing expressions to the variance (for
\theta\ll 1/\sqrt{n}
, see Figure
3.1
) and have to be dealt with seperately. As will be seen later, these are exactly the terms which drive the martingale part in the limiting distribution. Hence, we exclude them in the following result on the moment bound and define for
\theta^{\prime}<\theta

\displaystyle Z_{k}^{j}(\theta^{\prime},\theta):=\log\left(\frac{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})}{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{I_{j,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}}

(3.9)

for
j\in\{1,3,4,5,6,7,9\}
, whereas

\displaystyle\begin{split}Z_{k}^{2}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)&:=\left[\log\left(\frac{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})}{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})}\right)-\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\right]\mathbbm{1}_{I_{2,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}},\\
Z_{k}^{8}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)&:=\left[\log\left(\frac{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})}{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})}\right)-\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\right]\mathbbm{1}_{I_{8,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}}.\end{split}

(3.10)

Furthermore, for each
j=1,\dots,9
, we set

\overline{Z}_{k}^{j}(\theta^{\prime},\theta):=\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[Z_{k}^{j}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\mid X_{(k-1)/n}\right].

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
0<k_{1}<k_{2}<\dots<k_{m}
,
d_{i}\in\mathbb{N}
with
d_{i}\leq D
and
j_{i}\in\{1,\dots,9\}
for all
i=1,\dots,m
. Moreover, let
K>0
,
-K/\sqrt{n}\leq\theta^{\prime}<\theta\leq K/\sqrt{n}
and
Y_{k}^{j}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\in\{Z_{k}^{j}(\theta^{\prime},\theta),\overline{Z}_{k}^{j}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\}
. Then there exists a constant
C=C(\alpha,\beta,m,D)>0
such that (denote
k_{0}=0
)

\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{m}\left|Y_{k_{i}}^{j_{i}}(\theta^{\prime},\theta)\right|^{d_{i}}\right]\leq Cn^{\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}}|\theta-\theta^{\prime}|^{\sum_{i=1}^{m}d_{i}}\prod_{i=1}^{m}\frac{1}{\sqrt{k_{i}-k_{i-1}}}.

The proof is deferred to Section
F
.

4
Proof of Proposition
1.2

The Markov property of the process compels the decomposition (
3.4
) into the martingale part
M_{n}(\theta)
and the drift term
B_{n}(\theta)
. As in classical M-estimation, the overall idea of the proof is to show that the (negative) drift
B_{n}(\theta)
dominates the stochastic fluctuation
M_{n}(\theta)
outside a
1/n
-neighborhood of the true parameter. While the probabilistic properties of the martingale part are reasonably compatible with this general framework, the randomness of the drift together with its decomposition into the disjoint regimes
\{X_{(k-1)/n}\leq\rho_{0}\}
,
\{\rho_{0}<X_{(k-1)/n}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta\}
, and
\{\rho_{0}+\theta>X_{(k-1)/n}\}
make the derivation of sufficiently tight (deterministic) bounds with sufficiently high probability rather involved. Therefore, the proof of
n
-consistency splits into three parts where the reasoning for each of them is quite different. Consecutively, we show as summarized in Figure
4.1
:

•

\boldsymbol{\hat{\rho}_{n}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}}(1)}
. For
\theta\notin[\rho_{0}-K,\rho_{0}+K]
for some
K>0
, the term
\mathcal{I}_{5}(\theta)
(see (
3.3
)) dominates the other parts of the log-likelihood in the following (informal) sense: For every
\epsilon>0
, there exists
K=K(\epsilon)
such that

\inf_{\theta\notin[\rho_{0}-K,\rho_{0}+K]}|\mathcal{I}_{5}(\theta)|\quad\textrm{ is large as compared to }\quad\sup_{\theta\notin[\rho_{0}-K,\rho_{0}+K]}\left|\ell_{n}(\theta)-\mathcal{I}_{5}(\theta)\right|

with probability
>1-\epsilon
eventually. Roughly speaking, the reason is that the stochastic order of
\mathcal{I}_{5}(\theta)
scales proportionately in
\theta
, whereas the stochastic order of all remaining terms is driven by the number of observations falling into different, but small intervals, respectively. The uniformity in this argument follows from the Hölder continuity of the sample path.

•

\boldsymbol{\sqrt{n}|\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}|=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}}(1)}
. In order to prove that the MLE is not outside a
1/\sqrt{n}
-neighborhood of
\rho_{0}
, we employ a slicing argument and decompose the still dominant term
\mathcal{I}_{5}(\theta)
into its drift and martingale part. As compared to the first step, arguing with the sample path regularity is not sufficiently tight any longer to attain a smaller surrounding than an
n^{-1/2+\epsilon}
-neighborhood of
\rho_{0}
. Instead, we employ a bracketing argument together with a discrete local time approximation (Lemma
G.1
).

•

\boldsymbol{n|\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}|=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}}(1)}
. To finally prove
n
-consistency of the MLE, we again employ a slicing argument in combination with a chaining relying on second moment bounds. At this time, it turns out that the thorough control of the drift
B_{n}(\theta)
on each slice is analytically highly challenging:
As soon as
|\theta-\rho_{0}|\asymp n^{-1/2}
,
all
terms
\mathcal{I}_{1}(\theta),\dots,\mathcal{I}_{9}(\theta)
are of the same stochastic order of magnitude. At the same time, the likelihood function
\ell_{n}(\theta)
cannot be expanded into a Taylor series due to its discontinuities in the parameter
\theta
. Although for each
j=1,\dots,9
and any
k=1,\dots,n
,

\theta\mapsto\log\left(p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})\Big/p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})\right)\mathbbm{1}_{I_{j,k}^{0,\theta}}

possesses a Taylor expansion in principle, we are facing the problem that the remainder terms in each of these expansions are of the same order as their leading terms.

–

In order to bridge the regime until the Taylor expansion is helpful, we observe that for each
k=1,\dots,n
,

\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\left.\log\left(\frac{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})}{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})}\right)\right|X_{(k-1)/n}\right]

equals the negative Kullback–Leibler divergence of the conditional distributions
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}+\theta}(X_{k/n}\in\cdot\mid X_{(k-1)/n})
and
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(X_{k/n}\in\cdot\mid X_{(k-1)/n})
. Therefore, Pinsker’s inequality provides an upper bound of the negative drift
B_{n}(\theta)
in terms of total variation which is further estimated by

B_{n}(\theta)\mathbbm{1}_{A}\leq-n^{3/2}|\theta|^{2}\zeta\mathbbm{1}_{A},

where
\zeta>0
is a constant and
A
an event with high probability. However, this estimate is by far too weak to reach the
1/n
-environment of
\rho_{0}
.

–

Once we enter the neighborhood of
\rho_{0}
where the Taylor expansion is meaningful, we get the sharper bound

B_{n}(\theta)\mathbbm{1}_{A^{\prime}}\leq-n|\theta|\zeta^{\prime}\mathbbm{1}_{A^{\prime}},

where
\zeta^{\prime}>0
is a constant and
A^{\prime}
an event with high probability. This mimics the triangular shape observed in Figure
1.1
.

Fig 4.1:
Route of proof of the
n
-consistency.

Before giving the complete proof, we define parameters
\gamma,\Gamma,\xi
and specify certain sets. All of those are given for fixed
\epsilon>0
which is suppressed in the notation. For this, we define
\overline{X}:=\sup_{s\leq 1}X_{s}
,
\underline{X}:=\inf_{s\leq 1}X_{s}
.

•

By the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality, it is easily seen that both
\overline{X}
and
\underline{X}
are stochastically bounded. Thus, there exists a constant
\Gamma
such that
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(A_{1})>1-\epsilon
for the event

\displaystyle A_{1}:=\left\{\rho_{0}-\Gamma<\underline{X},\overline{X}<\rho_{0}+\Gamma\right\}.

(4.1)

•

Define
B_{k}=\{L_{1}^{y}(X)>0\textrm{ for all }y\in[\rho_{0}-1/k,\rho_{0}+1/k]\}
. Then
B_{k}\subset B_{k+1}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and by continuity of measures from below, we have
\lim_{K\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(\bigcup_{k=1}^{K}B_{k})=\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0)
. Consequently, for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
K_{\epsilon}
such that

\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\left.\bigcup_{k=1}^{K_{\epsilon}}B_{k}\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)=\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(B_{K_{\epsilon}}|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)>1-\epsilon,

and on
B_{K_{\epsilon}}
we have
L_{1}^{y}(X)>0
for all
y\in[\rho_{0}-1/K_{\epsilon},\rho_{0}+1/K_{\epsilon}]
which by Corollary
29.18
in
Kallenberg (2021)
gives
\overline{X}\geq\rho_{0}+1/K_{\epsilon}
and
\underline{X}\leq\rho_{0}-1/K_{\epsilon}
on
B_{K_{\epsilon}}
. Thus, defining
\gamma:=1/K_{\epsilon}
, the probability of

\displaystyle A_{2}:=\left\{\overline{X}\geq\rho_{0}+\gamma\textrm{ and }\underline{X}\leq\rho_{0}-\gamma\right\}

(4.2)

satisfies
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(A_{2}|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0)>1-\epsilon
.

•

Because
L_{1}^{\cdot}(X)
has a continuous version (as
X
is a continuous martingale) and every continuous function on a compact set attains its minimum and maximum, Corollary
29.18
in
Kallenberg (2021)
about range and support of continuous local martingales reveals

\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\left.\inf_{y\in[\rho_{0}-\gamma/2,\rho_{0}+\gamma/2]}L_{1}^{y}(X)>0\ \right|A_{2}\right)=1.

Again, by continuity of measures from below (analogously to the previous argument), we conclude the existence of
\xi>0
such that
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(A_{3}\mid A_{2})>1-\epsilon
, where

\displaystyle A_{3}=\left\{\inf_{y\in[\rho_{0}-\gamma/2,\rho_{0}+\gamma/2]}L_{1}^{y}(X)>\xi\right\}.

(4.3)

•

We define

\displaystyle A_{4}(n):=\left\{\sup_{|t-s|<1/n}|X_{t}-X_{s}|\leq n^{-4/9}\right\}.

(4.4)

Then by Markov’s inequality and Theorem
1
in
Fischer and Nappo (2010)
there exists a constant
\tilde{C}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{|t-s|<1/n}|X_{t}-X_{s}|>n^{-4/9}\right)

\displaystyle\leq n^{4/9}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\sup_{|t-s|<1/n}|X_{t}-X_{s}|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\tilde{C}n^{-1/18}\sqrt{\log(2n)}\longrightarrow 0.

Consequently, there exists
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
n\geq n_{0}
we have
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(A_{4}(n))>1-\epsilon
.

4.1
The MLE is not outside a
1/\sqrt{n}
-neighborhood of
\rho_{0}

In this subsection, we will prove that

\displaystyle\left|\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}\right|\mathbbm{1}_{\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}}=\mathcal{O}_{\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\right).

(4.5)

For the proof of this
\sqrt{n}
-consistency, recall the normalized log-likelihood
\ell_{n}(\theta)
given in (
1.5
) and fix an arbitrary
\epsilon>0
. As
\ell_{n}(0)=0
and
n(\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0})\in\mathrm{Argsup}_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\ell_{n}(z/n)
, we know that
|\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}|>K/\sqrt{n}
implies that
\sup_{|\theta|>K/\sqrt{n}}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0
. Consequently, we are going to show that

\displaystyle\limsup_{K\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sqrt{n}|\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}|>K,L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\leq\limsup_{K\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{\sqrt{n}|\theta|>K}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)<\epsilon.

To this aim, we further split

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{\sqrt{n}|\theta|>K}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{\sqrt{n}\theta>K}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)+\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{-\sqrt{n}\theta>K}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)

and only discuss the first summand as the second one can be dealt with analogously. The key idea is now to decompose
\ell_{n}(\theta)
into a dominant term that is part of
\mathcal{I}_{5}(\theta)
and a remainder that includes all other
\mathcal{I}_{j}(\theta)
,
j\neq 5
, and can be bounded independently of
\theta
(see Lemma
4.1
). To this aim, we define the two quantities

N_{n}^{L}(\theta):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left[\log\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\right)-\frac{(X_{k/n}-X_{(k-1)/n})^{2}}{2/n}\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}-\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\right)\right]\mathbbm{1}_{\{\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}\leq X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+\theta\}}

and its compensator

\displaystyle\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left[\log\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\right)-\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\left.\frac{(X_{k/n}-X_{(k-1)/n})^{2}}{2/n}\right|X_{(k-1)/n}\right]\left(\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}-\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\{\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}\leq X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+\theta\}},

where the parameter
L
will be specified later. We then have the following result (its proof can be found in Section
G.1
in the Appendix):

Lemma 4.1
.

Let
K,L>1
,
\epsilon>0
and
\Theta_{n}^{1}:=[K/\sqrt{n},n^{-1/4}]
,
\Theta_{n}^{2}:=(n^{-1/4},\infty)
. Then there exists a sequence of sets
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(A_{n}^{c}|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0)\leq\epsilon
for
n\geq n_{0}
, such that for
i=1,2
,

\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{n}^{i}}\ell_{n}(\theta)\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}\leq\sup_{\theta\in\Theta_{n}^{i}}N_{n}^{L}(\theta)\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}+F_{n}^{i}(K,L),

where
F_{n}^{i}(K,L)\geq 0
and
\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}[F_{n}^{1}(K,L)n^{-1/2}]\leq C_{\alpha,\beta}L<\infty
,
\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}[F_{n}^{2}(K,L)n^{-2/3}]\leq C_{\alpha,\beta}L<\infty
with a constant
C_{\alpha,\beta}
independent of both
K,L
and
n
.

Note that
\Theta_{n}^{1}
and
\Theta_{n}^{2}
in Lemma
4.1
are disjoint and
\Theta_{n}^{1}\cup\Theta_{n}^{2}=\{\theta:\ \theta\geq K/\sqrt{n}\}
. Furthermore, it should be noted that the moment bound on
F_{n}^{1}
of order
n^{1/2}
is optimal and the technically most involved part of the proof. It is based on a bound in
L^{2}(\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}})
on the error of discretely approximating an occupation times integral (see Lemma
G.1
) and a bracketing argument (see Lemma
G.2
). The order
n^{2/3}
for
F_{n}^{2}
is not optimal, but sufficient for our purpose and derived using only knowledge about the expected modulus of continuity of the path
X
. To proceed, we recall the sets
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3}
and
A_{4}(n)
given in (
4.1
)-(
4.4
) and let the constants be specified in such a way that the bounds of their respective probabilities are given for
\epsilon/10
(instead of
\epsilon
). Furthermore, we introduce two additional events:

•

Recall
F_{n}^{1}(K,L)
and
F_{n}^{2}(K,L)
from Lemma
4.1
and define

A_{5}(n):=\left\{|F_{n}^{1}(K,L)|\leq C_{F}L\sqrt{n},\ |F_{n}^{2}(K,L)|\leq C_{F}Ln^{2/3}\right\},

where
C_{F}>0
is chosen large enough such that
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(A_{5}(n))>1-\epsilon/10
for all
n\geq n_{1}
and appropriate
n_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
. This is possible by the tightness of
(F_{n}^{1}(K,L)/(Ln^{1/2}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(F_{n}^{2}(K,L)/(Ln^{2/3}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
implied by the moment bound in Lemma
4.1
.

•

Let
K>L
. Then, by Lemma
3.2
, the random variable

\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}\leq X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+K/\sqrt{n}\}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}g\left(\sqrt{n}(X_{(k-1)/n}-\rho_{0})\right)

with
g(x)=\mathbbm{1}_{[L,K)}(x)
converges in probability to
(K-L)\beta^{-2}L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)
. In particular, this random variable is bounded from below by
(K-L)\beta^{-2}\xi
on the set
A_{3}
. Consequently, there exists
n_{2}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(A_{6}(n)|A_{3})>1-\epsilon/10
for
n\geq n_{2}
, where

A_{6}(n):=\left\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}<X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+K/\sqrt{n}\}}>\left(K-L\right)\beta^{-2}\xi/2\right\}.

Finally, we define

A(n)=A_{1}\cap A_{2}\cap A_{3}\cap A_{4}(n)\cap A_{5}(n)\cap A_{6}(n)\cap A_{7}(n),

where
A_{7}(n)
is the set
A_{n}
in Lemma
4.1
for
\epsilon/10
. Then by the findings above we have for
n\geq\max\{n_{0},n_{1},n_{2}\}
large enough that (suppressing the index
n
in the events)

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}\cap A(n)^{c})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(A(n)^{c}|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0)\sum_{j=1}^{7}\mathbb{P}(A_{j}^{c}|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0)\leq\epsilon,

where the last inequality uses the probability bounds on
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(A_{j}^{c})
for
j=1,4,5
,
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(A_{3}^{c}|A_{2})
,
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(A_{6}^{c}|A_{3})
and
\mathbb{P}(A_{k}^{c}|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0)
for
k=2,7
together with

\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(A_{6}(n)^{c}|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(A_{6}(n)^{c}|A_{3}\right)+\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(A_{3}^{c}|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)

and

\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(A_{3}^{c}|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)\leq\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(A_{3}^{c}|A_{2}\right)+\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(A_{2}^{c}|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right).

Both of the last estimates follow by the law of total probability applied to
\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0)
and noting that
A_{2},A_{3}\subset\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}
.
After those preliminaries, we now start with the main part of the proof of (
4.5
), which is split into three parts.

Slicing argument

Subsequently, comma-separated lists within probabilities should be read as the intersection of the corresponding subsets of
\Omega
. For
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we define sets
S_{n,j}
via

S_{n,j}:=\left\{\theta:2^{j}<\sqrt{n}\theta\leq 2^{j+1}\right\}.

Then for
n\geq\max\{n_{0},n_{1}\}
and
K=2^{M}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{\sqrt{n}\theta>2^{M}}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq M\\
2^{j}\leq\Gamma\sqrt{n}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,A(n)\right)+\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{\theta>\Gamma}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,A(n)\right)\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt+\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(A(n)^{c}\cap\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}\right)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq M\\
2^{j}\leq\Gamma\sqrt{n}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,A(n)\right)+\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(N_{n}^{L}(\Gamma)+F_{n}^{2}(K,L)\geq 0,A(n)\right)+\epsilon,\end{split}

(4.6)

where the last step uses Lemma
4.1
and
N_{n}(\theta)=N_{n}(\Gamma)
for all
\theta\geq\overline{X}-\rho_{0}
, together with
\overline{X}-\rho_{0}\leq\Gamma
on
A(n)
. In what follows, we are going to analyze the first two of these summands and show that they vanish as
M\to\infty
, uniformly in
n
. With Lemma
4.1
and

F_{n,j}(K,L):=F_{n}^{1}(K,L)\mathbbm{1}_{\{2^{j}\leq n^{-1/4}\}}+F_{n}^{2}(K,L)\mathbbm{1}_{\{2^{j}>n^{-1/4}\}},

we find for the first summand in (
4.6
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,A(n)\right)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\left(N_{n}^{L}(\theta)+F_{n,j}(K,L)\right)\geq 0,A(n)\right)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\left(N_{n}^{L}(\theta)-\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)\right)\geq-\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)-|F_{n,j}(K,L)|,A(n)\right).\end{split}

(4.7)

Next, we will find a lower bound for the right-hand side in this probability, in particular for
\sup_{\theta}\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)
, on the set
A(n)
. Here, the problem is that
\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)
is still random and not necessarily negative. However, we will subsequently prove the following:

Claim I:
\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)<0
for large values of
L
.

Claim II: The subsequent inequalities (
4.8
) and (
4.10
), meaning heuristically that
\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)
scales (almost) at least proportionately in
\theta
.

The heuristic reason for Claim I is that
\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}[n(X_{k/n}-X_{(k-1)/n})^{2}\mid X_{(k-1)/n}]\approx\beta^{2}
for large values of
L
(meaning
X_{(k-1)/n}
being not too close to the change point
\rho_{0}
).
In this case, each summand appearing in the definition of
\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)
is negative, in particular

\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)\approx c_{\alpha,\beta}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{[\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n},\rho_{0}+\theta)}(X_{(k-1)/n})

for some negative constant
c_{\alpha,\beta}<0
. According to this,
\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)
is given approximately as a multiple of the number of observations falling into the interval
[\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n},\rho_{0}+\theta)
, which heuristically explains Claim II.

Now we give the details and start with Claim I. Its heuristic is made precise in the following preliminary lemma. The proof is deferred to Section
G.1
in the Appendix.

Lemma 4.2
.

For every
\epsilon>0
there exists
L=L(\epsilon)>0
such that for all
1\leq k\leq n
and every
\theta>L/\sqrt{n}
,

\left|\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\left.n(X_{k/n}-X_{(k-1)/n})^{2}\right|X_{(k-1)/n}\right]-\beta^{2}\right|\mathbbm{1}_{\{\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}\leq X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+\theta\}}\leq\epsilon.

First, take
\epsilon_{0}>0
to be small enough such that

d_{\alpha,\beta}:=\sup_{|\delta|<\epsilon_{0}}\left(\log\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\right)-\frac{1+\delta}{2}\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}-1\right)\right)<0,

which is possible since
\log(\beta/\alpha)-(\beta^{2}/\alpha^{2}-1)/2<0
for all
\alpha\neq\beta
. This follows from the fact that
f(x):=\log(x)-x^{2}/2+1/2
has a unique maximum at
x=1
with
f(1)=0
. Using Lemma
4.2
we choose
L=L(\epsilon_{0})
large enough such that

\displaystyle\left(\log\left(\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\right)-\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\left.\frac{(X_{k/n}-X_{(k-1)/n})^{2}}{2/n}\right|X_{(k-1)/n}\right]\frac{\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2}}{\alpha^{2}\beta^{2}}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}\leq X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+\theta\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt<d_{\alpha,\beta}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}\leq X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+\theta\}}

for
k=1,\dots,n
. Note that this choice of
L
is independent of
K
, which is important as in the definition of
A_{6}(n)
we require
K>L
. Claim I now follows by summation over
k
.

Building on these results, we now establish Claim II. Here, we have (recall
d_{\alpha,\beta}<0
)

\displaystyle-\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\overline{N}_{n}(\theta)

\displaystyle\geq-\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}d_{\alpha,\beta}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}\leq X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+\theta\}}

\displaystyle=-d_{\alpha,\beta}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}\leq X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+2^{j}/\sqrt{n}\}}

To continue bounding
\sup_{\theta}\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)
on
A(n)
and establish Claim II, we distinguish the two cases
2^{j}\leq n^{1/4}
and
2^{j}>n^{1/4}
when counting the observations in the interval
[\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n},\rho_{0}+2^{j}/\sqrt{n})
. The reason for treating these two cases separately is that for small values of
2^{j}
, it is enough to simply consider the smaller interval
[\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n},\rho_{0}+K/\sqrt{n})
, see (
4.8
). This, however, is not sharp enough for larger values of
2^{j}
, where we need to consider that the number of observations within a certain interval scales with its length with high probability. Once the order (in
n
) of the length of the interval
[\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n},\rho_{0}+2^{j}/\sqrt{n})
is larger than the modulus of continuity of the path
X
, we can switch from the sum of indicators (that count observations in the interval) to an occupation integral over a slighly smaller domain (but with length of the same order), see (
4.9
). Now we make these heuristics precise: For
2^{j}\leq n^{1/4}
, we simply use that
j\geq M
, meaning that
\mathbbm{1}_{\{\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}\leq X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+2^{j}/\sqrt{n}\}}\geq\mathbbm{1}_{\{\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}\leq X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+2^{M}/\sqrt{n}\}}
and consequently on
A_{6}(n)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\overline{N}_{n}(\theta)&\geq-d_{\alpha,\beta}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbbm{1}_{\{\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}\leq X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+2^{M}/\sqrt{n}\}}\\
&\geq-d_{\alpha,\beta}\sqrt{n}\left(2^{M}-L\right)\beta^{-2}\xi/2.\end{split}

(4.8)

For
2^{j}>n^{1/4}
, we make use of
A_{4}(n)
being part of
A(n)
. We first observe that on
A(n)
,

\int_{(k-1)/n}^{k/n}\mathbbm{1}_{[\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}+n^{-4/9},\rho_{0}+2^{j}/\sqrt{n}-n^{-4/9}]}(X_{s})ds\neq 0

implies
\mathbbm{1}_{\{\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}\leq X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}+2^{j}/\sqrt{n}\}}=1
. Together with the occupation times formula,

\displaystyle\begin{split}-\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\overline{N}_{n}(\theta)&\geq(-d_{\alpha,\beta})n\int_{0}^{1}\mathbbm{1}_{[\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}+n^{-4/9},\rho_{0}+2^{j}/\sqrt{n}-n^{-4/9}]}(X_{s})ds\\
&\geq\frac{(-d_{\alpha,\beta})n}{\max\{\alpha^{2},\beta^{2}\}}\int_{\rho_{0}+L/\sqrt{n}+n^{-4/9}}^{\rho_{0}+(2^{j}/\sqrt{n}-n^{-4/9})\wedge\gamma/2}L_{1}^{y}(X)dy\\
&\geq\frac{(-d_{\alpha,\beta})\xi n}{\max\{\alpha^{2},\beta^{2}\}}\left(\big(2^{j}/\sqrt{n}-2n^{-4/9}-L/\sqrt{n}\big)\wedge\big(\gamma/2-L/\sqrt{n}-n^{-4/9}\big)\right),\end{split}

(4.9)

where the the parameter
\gamma
appears in the definition of the set
A_{2}
and the second-last line follows from the fact that we work on
A_{3}
. For
2^{j}>n^{1/4}
and
n
large enough,

\frac{2^{j}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(1-2^{-(j-1)}n^{1/18}-2^{-j}L\right)\geq\frac{2^{j}}{\sqrt{n}}\left(1-2n^{1/18-1/4}-Ln^{-1/4}\right)\geq 2^{j-1}/\sqrt{n},

such that (
4.9
) gives

\displaystyle-\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\overline{N}_{n}(\theta)\geq\frac{(-d_{\alpha,\beta})\xi}{\max\{\alpha^{2},\beta^{2}\}\textasciiacute}n\left(2^{j-1}/\sqrt{n}\wedge\gamma/4\right).

(4.10)

Together with (
4.8
), this establishes Claim II.

Now we combine everything for the first summand in (
4.6
). With (
4.7
), the lower bound (
4.8
) for
2^{j}\leq n^{1/4}
, the bound (
4.10
) for
2^{j}>n^{1/4}
and the bound on
F_{n}^{i}(K,L)
on
A_{5}
we find for
M,n
large enough with Markov’s inequality

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq M\\
2^{j}\leq\Gamma\sqrt{n}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,A(n)\right)\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq M\\
2^{j}\leq n^{1/4}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\left|N_{n}^{L}(\theta)-\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)\right|\right]\frac{1}{C_{1}\sqrt{n}(2^{M}-L)-C_{F}\sqrt{n}}\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq M\\
n^{1/4}<2^{j}\leq\Gamma\sqrt{n}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\left|N_{n}^{L}(\theta)-\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)\right|\right]\frac{1}{C_{1}n(1\wedge 2^{j}/\sqrt{n})-C_{F}Ln^{2/3}},\end{split}

(4.11)

where
C_{1}=C_{1}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\xi,\Gamma)>0
is some universal constant.

Evaluating the expectation: Modified chaining

It remains to evaluate the expectation of the supremum in the last display which is done with a modified chaining procedure. It should be noted that the chaining will be interrupted when
N_{n}^{L}(\theta)-\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)
is compared for values
\theta,\theta^{\prime}
with difference of order
1/n
and the resulting remainder is treated in essence using variance bounds. This strategy is tailor-made to the special structure of the martingale term
N_{n}^{L}(\theta)-\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)
that allows to deduce tight bounds for the remainder that are not available in general. The details are given in Appendix
G.1
and yield the bound

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\left(N_{n}^{L}(\theta)-\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\theta)\right)\right]\leq C_{2}2^{(j+1)/2}n^{1/4}\left(j\log(2)+\log(n)\right),

(4.12)

where
C_{2}=C_{2}(\alpha,\beta)>0
is a suitable constant.

Finalizing the proof of (
4.5
)

From (
4.11
), we obtain the following bound for the first summand in (
4.6
):

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq M\\
2^{j}\leq\Gamma\sqrt{n}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{\theta\in S_{n,j}}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,A(n)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq M\\
2^{j}\leq n^{1/4}\end{subarray}}\frac{C_{2}2^{(j+1)/2}n^{1/4}\left(j\log(2)+\log(n)\right)}{C_{1}\sqrt{n}(2^{M}-L)-C_{F}\sqrt{n}}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq M\\
n^{1/4}<2^{j}\leq\Gamma\sqrt{n}\end{subarray}}\frac{C_{2}2^{(j+1)/2}n^{1/4}\left(j\log(2)+\log(n)\right)}{C_{1}n(1\wedge 2^{j}/(2\sqrt{n}))-C_{F}Ln^{2/3}}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq C_{3}n^{-1/8}(\log(n))^{2}+C_{3}\sum_{j\geq M}j2^{-j/2},

for a constant
C_{3}=C_{3}(\alpha,\beta,\gamma,\xi,\Gamma)>0
and
n
large enough. To complete the discussion for the first summand in (
4.6
), we now make the choice of
M
by taking it large enough to satisfiy
C_{4}\sum_{j\geq M}j2^{-j/2}<\epsilon
and then to take
n
large enough to have the first summand also
<\epsilon
.

It remains to deal with the second summand in (
4.6
). By the same argument as used for (
4.10
) we find

-\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\Gamma)\geq-nd_{\alpha,\beta}\xi\gamma/2

on
A(n)
and consequently,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(N_{n}^{L}(\Gamma)+F_{n}^{2}(K,L)\geq 0,A(n)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(N_{n}^{L}(\Gamma)-\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\Gamma)\geq-\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\Gamma)-|F_{n}^{2}(L,K)|,A(n)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[(N_{n}^{L}(\Gamma)-\overline{N}_{n}^{L}(\Gamma))^{2}\right]}{\left[C_{\alpha,\beta}(\xi,\gamma)n-C_{\alpha,\beta}(L,K)n^{2/3}\right]^{2}}\leq\frac{Cn\Gamma}{\left[C_{\alpha,\beta}(\xi,\gamma)n-C_{\alpha,\beta}(L,K)n^{2/3}\right]^{2}},

which converges to zero for
n\to\infty
. Thus, the first summands in (
4.6
) are shown to converge to zero and (
4.5
) follows.

4.2
The MLE is not outside a
1/n
-neighborhood of
\rho_{0}

In this subsection, we will complete the proof of Proposition
1.2
by proving
n
-consistency of the MLE
\hat{\rho}_{n}
. We will show tightness of
n|\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}|
by proving that

\displaystyle\lim_{M\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(n|\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}|>2^{M},L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)=0.

(4.13)

The proof again combines slicing and chaining techniques and will exploit the fact that we already established
\sqrt{n}
-consistency of
\hat{\rho}_{n}
which allows to consider only
\theta\leq K/\sqrt{n}
for suitable
K>0
. At this point it is crucial that (almost) sharp upper bounds on the drift term
B_{n}(\theta)
are needed and the next lemma provides those for
|\theta|<K/\sqrt{n}
. Note that the
n|\theta|
estimate for small
\theta
is needed to describe the triangular shape observed in Figure
1.1
sufficiently accurate to archive the
1/n
rate of convergence. However, as described in the beginning of Section
4
, a (Taylor) expansion of the log-likelihood function in each regime is only helpful for all
|\theta|\leq\kappa_{0}/\sqrt{n}
with
\kappa_{0}>0
small enough. For all other
\kappa_{0}/\sqrt{n}\leq\theta\leq K/\sqrt{n}
, we rely on the observation that the negative drift
B_{n}
is given by the sum over certain Kullback–Leibler divergences which are then bounded using Pinsker’s inequality.

Lemma 4.3
.

Let
K>0
. Then for every
\epsilon>0
there exist constants
\kappa_{0},\zeta>0
,
n_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
and a sequence of sets
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
with
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(A_{n}^{c}|L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0)\leq\epsilon
for
n\geq n_{0}
such that

B_{n}(\theta)\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}\leq\begin{cases}-n|\theta|\zeta\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}},&\textrm{ if }|\theta|\leq\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\sqrt{n}},\\
-n^{3/2}|\theta|^{2}\zeta\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}},&\textrm{ if }\frac{\kappa_{0}}{\sqrt{n}}<|\theta|\leq K/\sqrt{n}.\end{cases}

We will only give a brief sketch of the proof, the details can be found in Appendix
G.2
.
First, we specify
\kappa_{0}
and
\zeta_{1}
such that by the first inequality is valid for
|\theta|\leq\kappa_{0}/\sqrt{n}
with
\zeta=\zeta_{1}
by using Proposition
3.1
. For
\kappa_{0}/\sqrt{n}\leq|\theta|\leq K/\sqrt{n}
, this expansion is not helpful as the remainder term (which is of the same order as the leading term) is too large. Denoting by
\mathbb{P}_{\rho,1/n}^{X_{(k-1)/n}}(\cdot)=\mathbb{P}_{\rho}(X_{k/n}\in\cdot\mid X_{(k-1)/n})
the distribution of
X_{k/n}
given
X_{(k-1)/n}
in our model with parameter
\rho
and by
KL(\mathbb{P}_{1},\mathbb{P}_{2})
the Kullback–Leibler divergence of two probability measures
\mathbb{P}_{1}
,
\mathbb{P}_{2}
, we observe

\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\left.\log\left(\frac{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})}{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}}(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n})}\right)\ \right|\ X_{(k-1)/n}\right]=-KL\left(\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0},1/n}^{X_{(k-1)/n}},\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}+\theta,1/n}^{X_{(k-1)/n}}\right).

An explicit evaluation of this Kullback–Leibler divergence is analytically highly challenging, in particular because expressions built on the first two regimes of the transition density (
3.1
) contain logarithms of sums. In order to find a form that is more convenient to work with in our setting, we apply the first Pinsker inequality (
Tsybakov (2009)
, Lemma
2.5
) and find an upper bound in terms of total variation. Due to Scheffé’s theorem (
Tsybakov (2009)
, Lemma
2.1
) the second inequality in the statement can then be deduced from the inequality

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left|p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta}(X_{(k-1)/n},y)-p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}}(X_{(k-1)/n},y)\right|dy\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt\geq C_{\alpha,\beta}^{1}\theta\sqrt{n}\exp\left(-\frac{(X_{(k-1)/n}-\rho_{0})^{2}}{\min\{\alpha^{2},\beta^{2}\}/n}-C_{\alpha,\beta}^{2}K\right),\end{split}

(4.14)

where
C_{\alpha,\beta}^{1},C_{\alpha,\beta}^{2}>0
are suitable constants independent of
n
and
\theta
.
∎

We return to the proof of (
4.13
). Let
\epsilon>0
be arbitrary and
K>0
large enough such that
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(\sqrt{n}|\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}|>K,L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0)<\epsilon
. This choice of
K
is possible by the
\sqrt{n}
-consistency established in Subsection
4.1
(see (
4.5
)). Moreover, let
\kappa_{0},\zeta>0
be the constants and
(A_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
the sequence of set introduced in Lemma
4.3
. By the definition of
\hat{\rho}_{n}
satisfying
n(\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0})\in\mathrm{Argsup}_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\ell_{n}(z/n)
and using
\ell_{n}(0)=0
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(n|\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}|>2^{M},L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\{n|\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}|>2^{M},|\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}|\leq K/\sqrt{n}\}\cap A_{n}\right)+\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}(A_{n}^{c}\cap\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\})\\
&\hskip 42.67912pt+\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(|\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0}|>K/\sqrt{n},L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\right)\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{2^{M}<n\theta\leq K\sqrt{n}}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,A_{n}\right)+\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{2^{M}<-n\theta\leq K\sqrt{n}}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,A_{n}\right)+2\epsilon.\end{split}

(4.15)

In what follows, we treat the first probability on the right-hand side, the second one can be dealt with analogously. With the shells

\overline{S}_{n,j}:=\left\{\theta\in\Theta:\ 2^{j}<n\theta\leq 2^{j+1}\right\}

for
n\in\mathbb{N}
and
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, we then bound by subadditivity of
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{2^{M}<n\theta\leq K\sqrt{n}}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,A_{n}\right)\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq M\\
2^{j}\leq K\sqrt{n}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{\theta\in\overline{S}_{n,j}}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,A_{n}\right).

(4.16)

To proceed, we note that

\displaystyle\left\{\sup_{\theta\in\overline{S}_{n,j}}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0\right\}=\left\{\sup_{\theta\in\overline{S}_{n,j}}\ell_{n}(\theta)-\sup_{\theta\in\overline{S}_{n,j}}B_{n}(\theta)\geq-\sup_{\theta\in\overline{S}_{n,j}}B_{n}(\theta)\right\}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\subset\left\{\sup_{\theta\in\overline{S}_{n,j}}\left(\ell_{n}(\theta)-B_{n}(\theta)\right)\geq-\sup_{\theta\in\overline{S}_{n,j}}B_{n}(\theta)\right\}=\left\{\sup_{\theta\in\overline{S}_{n,j}}M_{n}(\theta)\geq-\sup_{\theta\in\overline{S}_{n,j}}B_{n}(\theta)\right\}.

Then (
4.16
) can be extended to

\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{2^{M}<n\theta\leq K\sqrt{n}}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,A_{n}\right)\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq M\\
2^{j}\leq K\sqrt{n}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{\theta\in\overline{S}_{n,j}}M_{n}(\theta)\geq-\sup_{\theta\in\overline{S}_{n,j}}B_{n}(\theta),A_{n}\right).

Next, we bound the supremum of
B_{n}(\theta)
over
\overline{S}_{n,j}
with the bounds provided in Lemma
4.3
where it is necessary to distinguish the cases where
2^{j}\leq\kappa_{0}\sqrt{n}
and
2^{j}>\kappa_{0}\sqrt{n}
. This gives on
A_{n}
the bounds

-\sup_{\theta\in\overline{S}_{n,j}}B_{n}(\theta)\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}=\inf_{\theta\in\overline{S}_{n,j}}\left(-B_{n}(\theta)\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}\right)\geq\begin{cases}2^{j}\zeta&\textrm{ for }2^{j}\leq\kappa_{0}\sqrt{n},\\
n^{-1/2}2^{2j}\zeta&\textrm{ for }2^{j}\geq\kappa_{0}\sqrt{n},\end{cases}

and consequently with Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0}}\left(\sup_{2^{M}<n\theta\leq K\sqrt{n}}\ell_{n}(\theta)\geq 0,A_{n}\right)&\leq\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq M\\
2^{j}\leq\kappa_{0}\sqrt{n}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\sup_{\theta\in\overline{S}_{n,j}}|M_{n}(\theta)|\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}\right]\frac{1}{\zeta 2^{j}}\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\geq M\\
\kappa_{0}\sqrt{n}<2^{j}\leq K\sqrt{n}\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\sup_{\theta\in\overline{S}_{n,j}}|M_{n}(\theta)|\mathbbm{1}_{A_{n}}\right]\frac{\sqrt{n}}{\zeta 2^{2j}}.\end{split}

(4.17)

In order to bound the expectations of the suprema appearing in the right-hand side of (
4.17
), we apply the modified chaining techique already mentioned in Subsection
4.1
. To this aim, we split
M_{n}(\theta)=M_{n}^{1}(\theta)+M_{n}^{2}(\theta)
into two parts, where

\displaystyle M_{n}^{1}(\theta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}<X_{k/n}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta\}}+\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\rho_{0}<X_{k/n}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta\leq X_{(k-1)/n}\}}

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\mathbb{E}_{\rho_{0}}\left[\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{X_{(k-1)/n}<\rho_{0}<X_{k/n}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta\}}\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 85.35826pt\left.\left.+\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\mathbbm{1}_{\{\rho_{0}<X_{k/n}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta\leq X_{(k-1)/n}\}}\right|X_{(k-1)/n}\right]

and
M_{n}^{2}(\theta)=M_{n}(\theta)-M_{n}^{1}(\theta)
. The martingale
M_{n}^{1}(\theta)
consists of parts of
\mathcal{I}_{2}(\theta)
and
\mathcal{I}_{8}(\theta)
that determine the order of the variance of
M_{n}(\theta)
, whereas all remaining terms are summarized in
